Discrete Lagrangian multiforms for quad equations II: tetrahedron and octahedron equations
Jacob J. Richardson, Mats Vermeeren

Abstract
We present four types of discrete Lagrangian 2-form associated to the integrable quad equations of the ABS list. These include the triangle Lagrangian that has traditionally been used in the Lagrangian multiform description of ABS equations, the trident Lagrangian that was central to Part I of this paper, and two Lagrangians that have not been studied in the multiform setting. Two of the Lagrangian 2-forms have the quad equations, or a system equivalent to the quad equations, as their Euler-Lagrange equations and one produces the tetrahedron equations. This is in contrast to the well-established Lagrangian 2-form for these equations, which produces equations that are weaker than the quad equations (they are equivalent to two octahedron equations). We use relations between the Lagrangian 2-forms to prove that the system of quad equations is equivalent to the combined system of tetrahedron and octahedron equations. Furthermore, for each of the Lagrangian 2-forms, we study the double zero property of the exterior derivative. In particular, this gives a possible variational interpretation to the octahedron equations.



1
Introduction

In Part I of this work
[
17
]
we revisited the discrete Lagrangian multiforms for the integrable quad equations of the ABS list. We showed that two central properties of the theory are more subtle than usually acknowledged:

(1)

the equivalence between quad equations and their three-leg forms,

(2)

the closure relation of the Lagrangian multiforms.

By introducing additional, integer-valued, fields to the action, we managed to put Property (1) on sound footing and recovered a slightly weaker version of Property (2). We gave counterexamples to the usual formulations of these properties.

We will review the three-leg forms involved in Property (1), and their importance to the Lagrangian structures, in Section
2
.
Property (2), the closure relation of Lagrangian multiform theory, deserves a few more words now. It states that, on solutions to the variational principle, the Lagrangian 2-form is closed. This means in particular that the action over a closed surface vanishes on solutions. For this property to hold on the ABS equations, we need to make a careful choice of the branches of the complex logarithms or dilogarithms that occur in the action functional. In Part I, we encoded this choice in additional terms in the action, involving integer-valued fields multiplied by
2\pi i
. For some of equations the ABS list, we showed that this action satisfies the closure relation. For the others, the closure relation holds up to a multiple of
4\pi^{2}
.

In Part I, we focused our attention to what we call the
trident Lagrangian
. We chose this over the established choice in discrete Lagrangian multiform theory (which we call the
triangle Lagrangian
and was introduced in
[
8
]
, see also
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
10
,
22
]
). Our motivation for this choice is that the trident Lagrangian produces Euler-Lagrange equations that are equivalent to the multi-affine quad equations, whereas the triangle Lagrangian produces a weaker set of equations. The first main result of Part II is to compare these choices of Lagrangian, as well as two further Lagrangians. We will show how the Euler-Lagrange equations of these different Lagrangian multiforms relate to various sets of equations that have been associated to integrable quad equations: tetrahedron equations and octahedron relations
[
4
,
5
]
.

The second main result of Part II involves an algebraic interpretation of the closure relation that has recently been emphasised: we can write the exterior derivative of the Lagrangian form as (a sum of) product(s) of Euler-Lagrange expressions. In other words, the exterior derivative is not just zero on solutions of the Euler-Lagrange equations (or possible a multiple of
4\pi^{2}
), but attains a
double zero
on this set of equations.
In the continuous (and semi-discrete) case, the double zero property has been used implicitly in
[
18
,
21
]
and discussed explicitly in
[
6
,
16
,
19
]
. In the discrete setting, a double zero expansion for the lattice Boussinesq equation was recently obtained in
[
14
]
. In the present work we give double zero expansions for the Lagrangian multiforms of the ABS list.
These double zero expansions extend the variational interpretation of the quad and tetrahedron equations and provides the first known variational interpretation of the octahedron equations.

The structure of this paper is as follows. In Section
2
we review the properties of the ABS equations that are relevant to this work. We start Section
3
with a short introduction to Lagrangian multiform theory and review the extension of the action by integer fields that was developed in Part I. In Sections
3.3
–
3.6
we present four types of Lagrangian multiform. For two of these, the variational principle produces exactly the set of quad equations. One of the Lagrangian multiforms produces only the tetrahedron equations. The final one is the well-known Lagrangian multiform on a triangular stencil. In Section
3.7
we discuss the closure relation of each of these 2-forms. In Section
4
we show that the relations between quad equations, tetrahedron equations, and octahedron equations follow from relations between the different Lagrangian 2-forms. In Section
5
we show that the exterior derivatives of all four 2-forms admit a double zero expansion (in terms of quad, tetrahedron, or octahedron polynomials). We give a general construction of these double zero expansions, as well as explicit expressions for the quad equation H1.

2
Quad equations

We consider the multi-affine and multidimensionally consistent quad equations from the ABS list
[
1
]
. The ABS list consists of

•

equations of Hirota-type H1, H2, H3
δ
,

•

more symmetric equations Q1
δ
, Q2, Q3
δ
, Q4,

•

equations A1
δ
, A2 that can be related to Q1 and Q3 by non-autonomous transformations.

The subscript in
Q1_{\delta}
etc. indicates dependence on a parameter
\delta
. We often distinguish between zero and nonzero values of the parameter by writing
Q1_{\delta=0}
or
Q1_{\delta\neq 0}
.
Details of these equations can be found for example in
[
1
,
3
,
7
,
12
]
, or in Part I
[
17
]
.

2.1
Three-leg forms

Given a field
u:\mathbb{Z}^{N}\to\mathbb{C}
and a reference point
\boldsymbol{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, we write
u=u(\boldsymbol{n}),u_{i}=u(\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{e}_{i}),u_{ij}=u(\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{e}_{i}+\boldsymbol{e}_{j})
, etc., where
e_{i}
denotes the unit vector in the
i
-th direction.
Each equation

Q_{ij}:=Q(u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=0

of the ABS list is related to a
three-leg form
. For some of the equations (H1, A1
δ=0
and Q1
δ=0
) there exist functions
\psi
and
\phi
such that the multi-affine equation is equivalent to

\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})=\psi(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})-\psi(u,u_{j},\alpha_{j})\,.

(1)

For the other quad equations, the most natural three-leg form has a multiplicative structure: there exist functions
\Psi
and
\Phi
such that the multi-affine equation is equivalent to

\Phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})=\frac{\Psi(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})}{\Psi(u,u_{j},\alpha_{j})}\,.

We can bring this into an additive form by taking the logarithm. Writing the logarithm of a quotient as the difference of logarithms may introduce an error of
2\pi i
. Setting
\psi=\log(\Psi)
and
\phi=\log(\phi)
, we find that the multi-affine equation (for H2, H3, A1
δ≠0
, A2, Q1
δ≠0
, Q2, Q3, Q4) is equivalent to

\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})=\psi(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})-\psi(u,u_{j},\alpha_{j})+2\Theta\pi i,\qquad\Theta\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(2)

For all equations of the ABS list, regardless of whether the three leg form is of type (
1
) or (
2
),
\phi
is an odd function of its last entry, i.e.
\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})=-\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i})
.

We denote the additive three-leg expression by

\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u)}:=\psi(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})-\psi(u,u_{j},\alpha_{j})-\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\,,

(3)

where the superscript indicates that the three legs meet at the vertex
u
. We can also consider three-leg forms based at the other three vertices of the square:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{i})}

\displaystyle:=\psi(u_{i},u_{ij},\alpha_{j})-\psi(u_{i},u,\alpha_{i})-\phi(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i})\,,

(4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{ij})}

\displaystyle:=\psi(u_{ij},u_{j},\alpha_{i})-\psi(u_{ij},u_{i},\alpha_{j})-\phi(u_{ij},u,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\,,

(5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{j})}

\displaystyle:=\psi(u_{j},u,\alpha_{j})-\psi(u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i})-\phi(u_{j},u_{i},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i})\,.

(6)

The functions
\phi
and
\psi
are independent of the choice of base vertex. This reflects the rotational symmetry of the multi-affine quad equation. The three-leg forms based at different vertices are illustrated in Figure
1
.

In summary, we have:

Proposition 1
.

For H1, A1
δ=0
and Q1
δ=0
, there holds

Q_{ij}=0\iff\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u)}=0\iff\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{i})}=0\iff\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{j})}=0\iff\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{ij})}=0\,.

For H2, H3, A1
δ≠0
, A2, Q1
δ≠0
, Q2, Q3, there holds

\displaystyle Q_{ij}=0

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u)}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i\iff\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{i})}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{j})}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i\iff\mathcal{Q}_{ij}^{(u_{ij})}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i\,.

The three-leg forms of quad equations on adjacent faces of the cube combine to form an equation on a tetrahedral stencil. Similar to the quad equation, we can base the three-leg form of the tetrahedron equation at any of its vertices, as illustrated in Figure
2
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u)}

\displaystyle:=\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u,u_{jk},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u,u_{ki},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})

(7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ij})}

\displaystyle:=\phi(u_{ij},u,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u_{ij},u_{ki},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u_{ij},u_{jk},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\,,

(8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ki})}

\displaystyle:=\phi(u_{ki},u_{jk},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u_{ki},u_{ij},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u_{ki},u,\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\,,

(9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u_{jk})}

\displaystyle:=\phi(u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u_{jk},u,\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u_{jk},u_{ij},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\,.

(10)

Figure 2:
Four three-leg forms of a tetrahedron equation.

The ABS equations of type Q have the property that their short and long leg functions are the same,
\phi=\psi
. Each equation of type H and A shares its long leg function
\phi
with an equation of type Q, but has a different short leg function
\psi
.
Thus, the tetrahedron equation of any member of the ABS list takes the form of a quad equation of type Q, in their respective three-leg forms. Similarly, the multi-affine tetrahedron equation
T=0
(equivalent to either
\mathcal{T}^{(u)}=0
or
\mathcal{T}^{(u)}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i
) can be described in terms of a quad polynomial of type Q, evaluated on a tetrahedron stencil:

T(u,u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=Q^{(\text{type Q})}(u,u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j},-\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{k})\,.

A second tetrahedron equation is found from this by point inversion,

T(u_{ijk},u_{k},u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=Q^{(\text{type Q})}(u_{ijk},u_{k},u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j},-\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{k})\,.

Its three-leg forms are:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ijk})}

\displaystyle:=\phi(u_{ijk},u_{k},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u_{ijk},u_{i},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u_{ijk},u_{j},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\,,

(11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u_{k})}

\displaystyle:=\phi(u_{k},u_{ijk},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u_{k},u_{j},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u_{k},u_{i},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\,,

(12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u_{j})}

\displaystyle:=\phi(u_{j},u_{i},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u_{j},u_{k},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u_{j},u_{ijk},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\,,

(13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u_{i})}

\displaystyle:=\phi(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u_{i},u_{ijk},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u_{i},u_{k},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\,.

(14)

The equivalences between the tetrahedron equations in multi-affine and three-leg forms are as follows:

Proposition 2
.

For H1, A1
δ=0
and Q1
δ=0
, the multi-affine equation

T(u,u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0

is equivalent to each of the following:

\mathcal{T}^{(u)}=0\iff\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ij})}=0\iff\mathcal{T}^{(u_{jk})}=0\iff\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ki})}=0\,.

For H2, H3, A1
δ≠0
, A2, Q1
δ≠0
, Q2, Q3,
he multi-affine equation

T(u,u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0

is equivalent to each of the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u)}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ij})}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{T}^{(u_{jk})}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ki})}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i\,.

The analogous equivalences relate
T(u_{ijk},u_{k},u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0
and
\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ijk})}
,
\mathcal{T}^{(u_{k})}
,
\mathcal{T}^{(u_{i})}
,
\mathcal{T}^{(u_{j})}
.

Example 1
.

For H1, also known as the lattice potential KdV equation, we have

Q_{ij}=(u_{i}-u_{j})(u-u_{ij})-\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}.

(15)

Its leg functions are given by

\displaystyle\psi(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})=u_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})=\frac{\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}}{u-u_{ij}},

so the three-leg form of the equation is
\mathcal{Q}^{(u)}_{ij}=0
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(u)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\psi(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})-\psi(u,u_{j},\alpha_{j})-\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})

\displaystyle=u_{i}-u_{j}-\frac{\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}}{u-u_{ij}}=\frac{Q_{ij}}{u-u_{ij}}\,.

The corresponding tetrahedron equation has three-leg form
\mathcal{T}^{(u)}=0
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(u)}

\displaystyle=\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\phi(u,u_{jk},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})+\phi(u,u_{ki},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}}{u-u_{ij}}+\frac{\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k}}{u-u_{jk}}+\frac{\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i}}{u-u_{ki}}

\displaystyle=\frac{T}{(u-u_{ij})(u-u_{jk})(u-u_{jk})}\,,

where
T
is the left-hand side of the multi-affine tetrahedron equation
T=0
,

T=(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})(uu_{ij}+u_{jk}u_{ki})+(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})(uu_{jk}+u_{ij}u_{ki})+(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})(uu_{ki}+u_{ij}u_{jk})\,.

(16)

3
Lagrangian multiforms

3.1
General theory

The central object of the Lagrangian multiform description of quad equations is a discrete two-form
\mathcal{L}(u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})
, i.e. a function that is skew-symmetric under the swap of indices
i\leftrightarrow j
. Instead of considering the action sum over a plane, Lagrangian multiform theory considers arbitrary 2-dimensional discrete surfaces in the lattice. Just like continuous differential 2-form can be integrated over any oriented surface, a discrete 2-form can be integrated over an oriented discrete surface by summing over all elementary squares making up the surface. We require that, for every choice of discrete surface, the corresponding action sum is critical with respect to variations of the field
u:\mathbb{Z}^{N}\to\mathbb{C}
. An equivalent characterisation is to require that the action sum over every elementary cube,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(u_{k},u_{ki},u_{jk},u_{ijk},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})+\mathcal{L}(u_{i},u_{ij},u_{ki},u_{ijk},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})+\mathcal{L}(u_{j},u_{jk},u_{ij},u_{ijk},\alpha_{k},\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathcal{L}(u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})-\mathcal{L}(u,u_{j},u_{k},u_{jk},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})-\mathcal{L}(u,u_{k},u_{i},u_{ki},\alpha_{k},\alpha_{i})\,,

is critical with respect to variations of the fields
u,u_{i},\ldots,u_{ijk}

[
4
]
.

Criticality with respect to variations of the fields leads to a set of generalised Euler-Lagrange equations, which we call
corner equations
. (They are also known as
multiform Euler-Lagrange equations
or
multi-time Euler-Lagrange equations
.) For the Lagrangians usually given for the ABS equations, the corner equations are linear combinations of the three-leg forms without
2\pi i
terms, as in Equation (
1
). To recover the three-leg equation modulo
2\pi i
, as in Equation (
2
), we introduced an extended action in Part I:

\displaystyle S^{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}}

\displaystyle=S+2\pi i(\Theta u+\Theta_{i}u_{i}+\Theta_{j}u_{j}+\Theta_{k}u_{k}+\Theta_{ij}u_{ij}+\Theta_{jk}u_{jk}+\Theta_{ki}u_{ki}+\Theta_{ijk}u_{ijk})

\displaystyle\qquad+2\pi i(\Xi_{i}\alpha_{i}+\Xi_{j}\alpha_{j}+\Xi_{k}\alpha_{k})\,,

where
\Theta,\Theta_{i},\ldots,\Theta_{ijk}
are integers associated to the vertices of the cube, and
\Xi_{i},\Xi_{j},\Xi_{k}
are integers associated to the lattice directions.

The terms in
S^{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}}
involving
\Xi_{i},\Xi_{j},\Xi_{k}
do not affect the corner equations, but they are key to the second variational principle in Lagrangian multiform theory: the action is invariant with respect to changes in the surface. In the discrete 2-form case, this is equivalent to the property that the action on each elementary cube vanishes, i.e.
S^{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}}=0
on solutions. Since this action can be thought of as the discrete exterior derivative of the Lagrangian 2-form, it is also referred to as the
closure relation
.

Similarly, for the equations H1, A1
δ=0
and Q1
δ=0
with a three-leg form of type (
1
), we consider

\displaystyle S^{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}

\displaystyle=S+2\pi i(\Xi_{i}\alpha_{i}+\Xi_{j}\alpha_{j}+\Xi_{k}\alpha_{k})

in order to have a closure relation.

3.2
Lagrangian leg functions

The discrete Lagrangian 2-forms we consider in this work are linear combinations of functions of two lattice sites only, which are often called the
legs
or
leg functions
.
After a suitable transformation of the variable
u
, there exist functions
L
and
\Lambda
such that the leg functions
\psi
and
\phi
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\psi(u,u_{i},\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{u}}L(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})\,,

\displaystyle\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{u}}\Lambda(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\,,

and also as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{u_{i}}}L(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\psi(u_{i},u,\alpha_{i})\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}\Lambda(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})

\displaystyle=\phi(u_{ij},u,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\,.

A key element in the ABS classification is provided by the biquadratics associated to a quad equation, i.e. the polynomials
h
and
g
satisfying

\displaystyle h(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})}\left(Q\frac{\partial{{}^{2}Q}}{\partial{u_{j}\partial u_{ij}}}-\frac{\partial{Q}}{\partial{u_{j}}}\frac{\partial{Q}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}\right),

\displaystyle g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})}\left(Q\frac{\partial{{}^{2}Q}}{\partial{u_{i}\partial u_{j}}}-\frac{\partial{Q}}{\partial{u_{i}}}\frac{\partial{Q}}{\partial{u_{j}}}\right),

where
k
is a skew-symmetric function of the lattice parameters, chosen such that
h
only depends on the indicated lattice parameter. They are related to the
L
and
\Lambda
by

[
3
, Lemma 3]

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{L(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\log h(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})+\kappa(u)+\kappa(u_{i})+c(\alpha_{i})\mod 2\pi i\,

(17)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\Lambda(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\log g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\kappa(u)+\kappa(u_{ij})-\gamma(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\mod 2\pi i\,,

(18)

for some functions
\kappa,c,\gamma
.

The quad equation
Q_{ij}=0
implies the following identities involving the biquadratics (see
[
3
, Lemma 1]
and
[
1
, Proposition 15]
):

\begin{split}h(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})h(u_{ij},u_{j},\alpha_{i})&=h(u,u_{j},\alpha_{j})h(u_{ij},u_{i},\alpha_{j})\\
&=g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\,.\end{split}

(19)

Similarly, the tetrahedron equations

T(u,u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad T(u_{ijk},u_{k},u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0

imply

\begin{split}g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u_{ki},u_{jk},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})&=g(u,u_{jk},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})g(u_{ki},u_{ij},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\\
&=g(u,u_{ki},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})g(u_{ij},u_{jk},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\end{split}

(20)

and

\begin{split}g(u_{ijk},u_{k},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u_{j},u_{i},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})&=g(u_{ijk},u_{i},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})g(u_{j},u_{k},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\\
&=g(u_{ijk},u_{j},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})g(u_{k},u_{i},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\end{split}

(21)

respectively.

3.3
Cross 2-form: tetrahedron equations are variational

We will show that the tetrahedron equations arise as corner equations of the following 2-form, which we call the
cross Lagrangian
,

\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})=\Lambda(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})-\Lambda(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\,.

The name for this Lagrangian is inspired on its leg structure, which is shown in Figure
3
(a). Note that the lattice is a bipartite graph, we can colour the vertices such that each edge links a black vertex with white vertex. Then each term of the cross Lagrangian involves two lattice sites of the same colour, so the cross Lagrangian (and its action) can be decomposed into one contribution from the black graph, and one from the white graph. These could be studied separately, as was done in
[
2
,
3
]
in the more general context of Laplace type equations on a bipartite quad graph.

(22)

\displaystyle=\Lambda(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})-\Lambda(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})-{\,\overleftrightarrow{\!(\cdots)\!}\,}+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\,,

where
{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}
denotes the expressions obtained from the preceding one by cyclic permutations, and
{\,\overleftrightarrow{\!(\cdots)\!}\,}
denotes the point inversion of the preceding terms, i.e. the expression obtained by interchanging
u\leftrightarrow u_{ijk},u_{i}\leftrightarrow u_{jk}
, etc.

\displaystyle=\phi(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}+2\Theta\pi i

and expressions obtained from these by cyclic permutation of the indices.

, there exist

such that these expressions are zero if and only if the multi-affine tetrahedron equation

holds.

To prove the second claim, we use Equations (

)–(

) to compute

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{S^{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\displaystyle=\log(g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}))-\log(g(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}))-{\,\overleftrightarrow{\!(\cdots)\!}\,}+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}+2\Xi_{i}\pi i

\displaystyle\equiv\log\!\left(\frac{g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u_{ki},u_{jk},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})}{g(u_{k},u_{ijk},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})}\right)+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\mod 2\pi i\,.

By virtue of Equations (

)–(

), the logarithm in this expression vanishes on the tetrahedron equations, so given a solution to the tetrahedron equations, we can choose
\Xi_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\frac{\partial{S^{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

.
∎

3.4
Trident 2-form: quad equations are variational

One of the main results of Part I was that the quad equations of the ABS list are variational. We showed that the trident Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 5.91 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}):=L(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})-L(u,u_{j},\alpha_{j})-\Lambda(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\,,

has corner equations that produce the quad equations directly (in their three-leg form).
This is illustrated in Figure
4
, where it can be seen that at each vertex there are three legs contributing to the action around the cube. These three legs either lie in a single quad, or span a tetrahedron. Correspondingly, varying the field at one vertex will either give a quad equation or a tetrahedron equation (in three-leg form). Since the tetrahedron equations are a consequence of the system of quad equations, we have the following result.

and Q1

, the quad equations are satisfied if and only if there exist

Proof.

We identify the corner equations with the three-leg forms of the quad equations (
3
)–(
6
) and tetrahedron equations (
7
) and (
11
) by observing that

\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 5.91 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}}}}{\partial{u}}=\mathcal{T}^{(u)}+2\Theta\pi i\,,\qquad\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 5.91 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(29)

where
\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{i})}_{jk}=\psi(u_{i},u_{ij},\alpha_{j})-\psi(u_{i},u_{ki},\alpha_{k})-\phi(u_{i},u_{ijk},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})
is the three leg form based at
u_{i}
in the quad oriented along the
j,k
-directions. Since the quad equations imply the tetrahedron equations, Equations (
29
) imply that the quad equations are equivalent to the system (
27
).

Similar to Proposition
3
, Equations (
28
) can be proved using the biquadratics associated to each quad equation. Details can be found in
[
17
]
.
∎

3.5
Cross-square 2-form

We introduce a second 2-form that produces a system of corner equations equivalent to the quad equations. The function defining this 2-form was introduced in
[
3
]
, where it was studied on a single quad. To our knowledge, it was never before considered as a Lagrangian multiform. It is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 5.91 0 L 0 5.91 L 0 0 L 5.91 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

\displaystyle:=L(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})+L(u_{ij},u_{j},\alpha_{i})-L(u,u_{j},\alpha_{j})-L(u_{ij},u_{i},\alpha_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad-\Lambda(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})-\Lambda(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})

Inspired by its leg structure, illustrated in Figure
5
, we call it the
cross-square Lagrangian
.

\displaystyle S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\displaystyle=2L(u_{i},u_{ij},\alpha_{j})+\Lambda(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\Lambda(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})-{\,\overleftrightarrow{\!(\cdots)\!}\,}+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\,,

\displaystyle=2S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Proof.

From (
3.5
) we deduce that the corner equations are a the corresponding linear combination of the corner equations (

) and (

)–(

) of the trident and cross Lagrangian 2-forms. Hence

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 5.91 0 L 0 5.91 L 0 0 L 5.91 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{u}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}^{(u)}+2\Theta\pi i\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 5.91 0 L 0 5.91 L 0 0 L 5.91 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{u_{ijk}}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{T}^{(u_{ijk})}+2\Theta_{ijk}\pi i\,,

(33)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 5.91 0 L 0 5.91 L 0 0 L 5.91 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{u_{i}}}

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{i})}_{jk}+\mathcal{T}^{(u_{i})}+2\Theta_{i}\pi i\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{}{}
{}{}

{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 5.91 0 L 0 5.91 L 0 0 L 5.91 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{u_{jk}}}

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{jk})}_{jk}-\mathcal{T}^{(u_{jk})}+2\Theta_{jk}\pi i\,,

where
\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{i})}_{jk}=\psi(u_{i},u_{ij},\alpha_{j})-\psi(u_{i},u_{ki},\alpha_{k})-\phi(u_{i},u_{ijk},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})
is the three leg form based at
u_{i}
in the quad oriented along the
j,k
-directions and
\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{j}k)}_{jk}
is as in Equation (
5
).
The remaining corner equations are obtained from (
33
) by cyclic permutation of the indices.

If multi-affine quad equations hold, then all of the three-leg forms involved in the system (
33
) are multiples of
2\pi i
, so the corner equations are satisfied. Conversely, if all equations of the system (
33
) hold, then in particular the tetrahedron equations in three-leg form, centred at
u
and
u_{ijk}
, are satisfied. Then the tetrahedron three-leg forms centred at
u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki}
also vanish modulo
2\pi i
, so the remaining corner equations reduce to individual quad equations in three-leg form. These are equivalent to the multi-affine quad equations.

Furthermore, using Equations (
17
)–(
18
), we find

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 5.91 0 L 0 5.91 L 0 0 L 5.91 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\log(h(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i}))+\log(h(u_{ij},u_{j},\alpha_{i}))

\displaystyle\quad-\log(g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}))-\log(g(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}))+2c(\alpha_{i})-2\gamma(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})

\displaystyle\equiv\log\!\left(\frac{h(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})h(u_{ij},u_{j},\alpha_{i})}{g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})}\right)+2c(\alpha_{i})-2\gamma(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\mod 2\pi i\,.

From Equation (
19
) it now follows that, on solutions to the quad equations,
\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 5.91 0 L 0 5.91 L 0 0 L 5.91 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}
is a multiple of
2\pi i
, so there exists a
\Xi_{i}
such that

\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The following lemma states that, for the cross-square Lagrangian, we can also obtain the quad equation by taking partial derivatives of the Lagrangian on a single quad. Lagrangian multiform theory does not require this property, but it will be a useful tool in Section
5.3
.

The action around the elementary cube of the triangle Lagrangian depends on an octahedral stencil depicted in Figure
6
,

\displaystyle S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\displaystyle=L(u_{i},u_{ij},\alpha_{j})+\Lambda(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})-{\,\overleftrightarrow{\!(\cdots)\!}\,}+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\,.

(34)

Since
u
and
u_{ijk}
does not appear in this action, there are no corner equations at these points.
The corner equation at
u_{ij}
in terms of leg functions is

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}\left(L(u_{i},u_{ij},\alpha_{j})-L(u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i})-\Lambda(u_{ij},u_{ki},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})-\Lambda(u_{jk},u_{ij},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\psi(u_{ij},u_{i},\alpha_{j})-\psi(u_{ij},u_{j},\alpha_{i})-\phi(u_{ij},u_{ki},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})-\phi(u_{ij},u_{jk},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})

(35)

\displaystyle=:\mathcal{E}_{ij}\,.

Note that this corner equation can be written as a combination of two quad equations in three-leg form, based at the vertex
u_{ij}
:

\mathcal{E}_{ij}=-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{ij})}_{jk}-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{ij})}_{ki}\,.

(36)

At the vertex
u_{i}
, we find the corner equation
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\mathcal{E}\,}\!_{ij}=0
, where
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\mathcal{E}\,}\!_{ij}:=-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{k})}_{jk}-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{k})}_{ki}
is obtained from
\mathcal{E}_{ij}
by point inversion in the cube, i.e.
u\leftrightarrow u_{ijk},u_{i}\leftrightarrow u_{jk}
, etc.

Proposition 7
.

For H2, H3, A1
δ≠0
, A2, Q1
δ≠0
, Q2, Q3, the equations
\mathcal{E}_{ij}=0,\mathcal{E}_{ij}=0,\ldots,\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\mathcal{E}\,}\!_{ki}=0
are satisfied if and only if there exist
\Theta,\Theta_{i},\ldots,\Theta_{ijk}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\Xi_{i},\Xi_{j},\Xi_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(38)

For H1, A1
δ=0
and Q1
δ=0
, the quad equations are satisfied if and only if there exist
\Xi_{i},\Xi_{j},\Xi_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that Equations (
37
)–(
38
) hold for
S^{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}
.

Proof.

Expressions (
37
) follow from equation (
35
) and its analogues obtained by cyclicity and point inversion.

To derive Equations (
38
), we first show that any solution
(u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki})
to the
\mathcal{E}
-equations can be extended to a solution to the quad equations. We have that

\displaystyle\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\mathcal{E}\,}\!_{ij}=-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{k})}_{jk}-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{k})}_{ki}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i\,,

\displaystyle\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\mathcal{E}\,}\!_{jk}=-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{i})}_{ki}-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{i})}_{ij}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i\,,

\displaystyle\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\mathcal{E}\,}\!_{ki}=-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{j})}_{ij}-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{j})}_{jk}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i\,.

Choose
u
such that the quad equation
Q_{ij}=0
holds. Then
\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{i})}_{ij}\equiv\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{j})}_{ij}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i
, hence
\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{i})}_{ki}\equiv\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{j})}_{jk}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i
, so the quad equations
Q_{jk}=0
and
Q_{ki}=0
hold as well. Similarly, we can choose
u_{ijk}
such that the three shifted quad equations
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{ij}=0
,
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{jk}=0
, and
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{ki}=0
hold.

Now, starting from the definition (
34
) and using Equations (
17
)–(
18
), we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{S^{\Theta,\Xi}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}\Big(L(u_{k},u_{ki},\alpha_{i})-L(u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i})+\Lambda(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})+\Lambda(u_{k},u_{i},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\Lambda(u_{ki},u_{jk},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})-\Lambda(u_{jk},u_{ij},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i}\Big)+2\Xi_{i}\pi i

\displaystyle\equiv\log\left(\frac{h(u_{k},u_{ki},\alpha_{i})g(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u_{jk},u_{ij},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})}{h(u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i})g(u_{k},u_{i},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})g(u_{ki},u_{jk},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})}\right)\mod 2\pi i

\displaystyle\equiv\log\left(\frac{g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})}{h(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})h(u_{j},u_{ij},\alpha_{i})}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\log\left(\frac{h(u,u_{i},\alpha_{i})h(u_{k},u_{ki},\alpha_{i})}{g(u,u_{ki},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})g(u_{k},u_{i},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\log\left(\frac{g(u_{jk},u_{ij},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})g(u,u_{ki},\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})}{g(u_{ik},u_{jk},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})g(u,u_{ij},\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})}\right)\mod 2\pi i\,.

Each of the logarithms vanishes on the quad equations, so the existence of a suitable
\Xi_{i}
follows.
∎

The triangle Lagrangian 2-form produces a set of corner equations that vanish on the quad equations but are not equivalent to them, because they lack the variables
u
and
u_{ijk}
. Furthermore, we can relate each of the four-leg equations (

) to a quad equation and a tetrahedron equation. From
\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

and thus

(40)

Example 2

(H1 Triangle Lagrangian)
.

For H1 we have

\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=uu_{i}-uu_{j}-(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\log(u_{i}-u_{j})\,,

so

\displaystyle S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

\displaystyle=u_{k}u_{ki}-u_{k}u_{jk}-(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\log(u_{ki}-u_{jk})+(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\log(u_{i}-u_{j})^{2}+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\,,

\displaystyle=u_{i}u_{ij}+(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\log(u_{i}-u_{j})-{\,\overleftrightarrow{\!(\cdots)\!}\,}+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}

Two corner equations are identically zero and the other six have a four-leg form. The corner equations at
u_{ij}
,
u_{jk}
and
u_{ki}
are all of the form

\displaystyle 0=\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}

\displaystyle=u_{i}-u_{j}-\frac{\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k}}{u_{ij}-u_{ki}}+\frac{\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i}}{u_{jk}-u_{ij}}=-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{ij})}_{jk}-\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{ij})}_{ki}=\mathcal{E}_{ij}\,.

Clearly these equations vanish on the quad equations. Similarly,
the other three corner equations are of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{u_{k}}}

\displaystyle=u_{ki}-u_{jk}+\frac{\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i}}{u_{k}-u_{i}}-\frac{\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k}}{u_{j}-u_{k}}=\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{k})}_{jk}+\mathcal{Q}^{(u_{k})}_{ki}=-\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\mathcal{E}\,}\!_{ij}\,.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle 0=\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\log(u_{i}-u_{j})-\log(u_{jk}-u_{ki})-\log(u_{k}-u_{i})+\log(u_{ij}-u_{jk})+2\Xi_{i}\pi i

\displaystyle\equiv\log\!\left(\frac{(u_{i}-u_{j})(u_{ij}-u_{jk})}{(u_{k}-u_{i})(u_{jk}-u_{ki})}\right)\mod 2\pi i\,.

The only way the principal branch of
\log
can yield a multiple of
2\pi i
is for it to be exactly zero. Hence,
\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}=0
is equivalent to the lattice KP equation
[
13
]

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(u_{i}-u_{j})(u_{ij}-u_{jk})-(u_{k}-u_{i})(u_{jk}-u_{ki})

\displaystyle=u_{i}(u_{ij}-u_{ki})+u_{j}(u_{jk}-u_{ij})+u_{k}(u_{jk}-u_{ki})\,.

In other words, Proposition
7
gives us a short proof that the
\mathcal{E}
-equations for H1 imply the discrete KP equation. We will return to this when we discuss octahedron equations in Section
4
.

3.7
Closure relation

Propositions
4
–
7
above provide the basis to prove closure of each of the Lagrangian multiforms, following the same approach we took in
[
17
]
for the trident Lagrangian. Propositions
4
–
7
state that on solutions of the corner equations, the gradient of the action with respect to both parameters
\alpha
and fields
u
vanishes. This means that perturbations of a given solution will have the same value of the action, unless it crosses a branch cut in one of the functions making up the action. For H1,H2,Q1,Q2,A1, the effect of crossing a branch cut is cancelled by the change in
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
(if present) and
\boldsymbol{\Xi}
that happens at the same point. For H3,Q3,A2, the net effect of crossing a branch cut is that the value of the action changes by a multiple of
4\pi^{2}
.

Thus we arrive at the following statement
[
17
, Theorem 8]
:

Theorem 8
.

Let
S
denote one of the actions
S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

,

,

, or

for H2, H3, A1
δ≠0
, A2, Q1

,

,

, or

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}
for H1, A1
δ=0
, Q1
δ=0
.

For any solution
\boldsymbol{u}
to the corner equations of
S
, with associated integer fields
\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}
, there holds

S(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi})=\begin{cases}0&\text{for H1, H2, Q1, Q2, and A1,}\\
4k\pi^{2}\quad(k\in\mathbb{Z})&\text{for H3, Q3, and A2.}\end{cases}

4
Tetrahedron and octahedron equations

Previously, we discussed the three-leg forms equivalent to the multi-affine quad and tetrahedron equations. In this section, we discuss the polynomial equations associated with the four-leg corner equations (
35
) of the triangle 2-form. We review the related multi-affine
octahedron equations
(also known as
octahedron relations
) and their relationship to quad equations. Most of this section is based on
[
5
]
, but in Proposition
10
we provide an additional variational interpretation of the relation between the different types of polynomial equations.

The four-leg equation
\mathcal{E}_{ij}=0
or
\mathcal{E}_{ij}\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i
, where the left-hand side is defined in Equation (
36
), can be obtained by eliminating the variable
u_{ijk}
from two quad equations in three-leg form. An equivalent equation is obtained by eliminating
u_{ijk}
from the corresponding two multi-affine quad equations. This yields the equation
E_{ij}=0
, where the left-hand side is the polynomial

E_{ij}=\frac{\partial{\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{jk}}}{\partial{u_{ijk}}}\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{ki}-\frac{\partial{\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{ki}}}{\partial{u_{ijk}}}\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{jk}\,,

(41)

where
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\cdot\,}\!
denotes point inversion in the cube, for example
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{jk}=Q(u_{ijk},u_{ki},u_{ij},u_{i},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})
.
We have two more polynomials by permuting indices and three more from point inversion:

\!\overleftrightarrow{\,E\,}\!_{ij}=\frac{\partial{Q_{jk}}}{\partial{u}}Q_{ki}-\frac{\partial{Q_{ki}}}{\partial{u}}Q_{jk}\,.

(42)

These polynomials lead to equations that are equivalent to those involving the four-leg expressions
\mathcal{E}_{ij}
:

E_{ij}(u_{i},u_{j},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0

is equivalent to

\mathcal{E}_{ij}(u_{i},u_{j},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0

for H1, Q1
δ=0
, A1
δ=0
, and to

\mathcal{E}_{ij}(u_{i},u_{j},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})\equiv 0\mod 2\pi i

for H2, H3, A1
δ≠0
, A2, Q1
δ≠0
, Q2, Q3.

From their definition as combinations of quad equations, it is clear that
E_{ij}=0
and
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,E\,}\!_{ij}=0
are consequences of the quad equations. However, they are not equivalent to the quad equations. Only two of these six equations are independent (see Proposition
9
below).

The relation (
40
) suggests that the polynomial
E_{ij}
is related to the polynomials
Q_{ij}
and
T
. Indeed, eliminating the variable
u
from the system
T=0
,
Q_{ij}=0
must lead to an equation equivalent to
E_{ij}=0
, so we find

\frac{\partial{Q_{ij}}}{\partial{u}}T-\frac{\partial{T}}{\partial{u}}Q_{ij}=\gamma(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})E_{ij}\,.

(43)

Here,
\gamma(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})
does not contain field variables, because the left hand side depends linearly on
(u_{i},u_{j},u_{jk},u_{ki})
and quadratically on
u_{ij}
, as does
E_{ij}
.
Under point inversion we find the analogous relation

\frac{\partial{\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{ij}}}{\partial{u_{ijk}}}\!\overleftrightarrow{\,T\,}\!-\frac{\partial{\!\overleftrightarrow{\,T\,}\!}}{\partial{u_{ijk}}}\!\overleftrightarrow{\,Q\,}\!_{ij}=\gamma(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})\!\overleftrightarrow{\,E\,}\!_{ij}\,.

The main result of
[
5
]
is that for every member of the ABS list there exist two octahedron equations which are equivalent to the set of
E
-equations. These are of the form

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}(u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0\,,

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=0\,,

where
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}
are multi-affine polynomials in the field variables
u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki}
, which form an octahedral stencil. These polynomials are (anti-)symmetric under point inversion:
For Q4 and A2 we have
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\Omega\,}\!_{1}=\Omega_{1}
and
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\Omega\,}\!_{2}=\Omega_{2}
; for the rest of ABS list there holds
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\Omega\,}\!_{1}=-\Omega_{1}
and
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\Omega\,}\!_{2}=\Omega_{2}
.

In all cases we can get
E
-polynomials by eliminating a variable form the system of octahedron equations. For Q4 and A2, eliminating
u_{k}
and
u_{ij}
leads to, respectively,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\Omega_{1}}}{\partial{u_{k}}}\Omega_{2}-\frac{\partial{\Omega_{2}}}{\partial{u_{k}}}\Omega_{1}

\displaystyle=\mu(u_{i},u_{j},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})E_{ij}\,,

(44)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\Omega_{1}}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}\Omega_{2}-\frac{\partial{\Omega_{2}}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}\Omega_{1}

\displaystyle=\mu(u_{jk},u_{ki},u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})\!\overleftrightarrow{\,E\,}\!_{ij}\,,

where the factor
\mu(u_{i},u_{j},u_{jk},u_{ki},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})
is polynomial in
u_{i},u_{j},u_{jk},u_{ki}
.
For the rest of ABS list, we have the explicit expressions

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\Omega_{1}}}{\partial{u_{k}}}\Omega_{2}-\frac{\partial{\Omega_{2}}}{\partial{u_{k}}}\Omega_{1}

\displaystyle=g_{1}\left(\phantom{-}\frac{\partial{\Omega_{1}}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}-\frac{\partial{\Omega_{1}}}{\partial{u_{k}}}\phantom{-}\right)\,E_{ij}\,,

(45)

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial{\Omega_{1}}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}\Omega_{2}+\frac{\partial{\Omega_{2}}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}\Omega_{1}

\displaystyle=g_{1}\left(-\frac{\partial{\Omega_{1}}}{\partial{u_{k}}}+\frac{\partial{\Omega_{1}}}{\partial{u_{ij}}}\phantom{-}\right)\,\!\overleftrightarrow{\,E\,}\!_{ij}\,,

where
g_{1}=g_{1}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})
is defined explicitly on a case by case basis in
[
5
, Proposition 5.3]
.

Example 3

(H1 octahedron equations)
.

For H1 the four-leg equations
\mathcal{E}_{ij}=0
and
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,\mathcal{E}\,}\!_{ij}=0
are equivalent to the following polynomials, respectively,

\displaystyle E_{ij}

\displaystyle=(u_{i}-u_{j})(u_{ij}-u_{jk})(u_{ij}-u_{ki})+\alpha_{i}(u_{ki}-u_{ij})+\alpha_{j}(u_{ij}-u_{jk})+\alpha_{k}(u_{jk}-u_{ki})\,,

\displaystyle\!\overleftrightarrow{\,E\,}\!_{ij}

\displaystyle=(u_{jk}-u_{ki})(u_{k}-u_{i})(u_{k}-u_{j})+\alpha_{i}(u_{j}-u_{k})+\alpha_{j}(u_{k}-u_{i})+\alpha_{k}(u_{i}-u_{j})\,.

These can written in terms of the following multi-affine octahedron polynomials

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}

\displaystyle=u_{i}(u_{ki}-u_{ij})+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\,,

(46)

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i}(u_{k}-u_{j}+u_{ij}-u_{ki})+u_{i}u_{jk}(u_{j}-u_{k}-u_{ij}u_{ki})+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\,,

(47)

Equation (
46
) is exactly the discrete KP equation we obtained earlier from
\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}=0
. The latter can be understood as a conservation law,

0=\frac{\partial{S}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}=\Delta_{k}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}_{ij}}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}+\Delta_{j}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}_{ki}}}{\partial{\alpha_{i}}}\,,

where
\Delta_{k}
denotes the difference between the shift in the
k
-direction and the function itself.
This is the 2-form version of the
spectrality property
that was formulated in
[
20
]
for discrete Lagrangian 1-forms.
Further investigation is required to determine how this property relates to the octahedron equations of
[
5
]
for other members of the ABS list.

The equivalence between the corner equations of
\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
(i.e. the
E
-equations obtained by setting the expressions (
41
) and (
42
) to zero) and octahedron equations
can be seen as a particular case of the following statement, which holds in the sense of fractional ideals, as explained in
[
5
]
.

Proposition 9
.

Out of the set of equations consisting of the six
E
-equations and two octahedron equations, any 2 equations imply the other six.

Further to this, a dimension-counting argument suggests that if we add the tetrahedron equations to the set of octahedron equations (or
E
-equations), we should obtain a system equivalent to the quad equations. The Lagrangian multiform structure provides an explicit proof of this fact:

Proposition 10
.

The tetrahedron equations and octahedron equations together are equivalent to the quad equations.

Proof.

From Equation (
7
) (or from tetrahedron property as assumed in the ABS classification
[
1
]
) it follows that the quad equations imply the tetrahedron equations. From (
44
) or (
45
) it follows that the
E
-equations, and hence the quad equations, imply the octahedron equations (in the sense of fraction ideals, as in
[
5
]
).

To prove the other implication, assume that the octahedron and tetrahedron equations are satisfied. Then the actions of

and

) are the six quad equations around the cube (together with the two tetrahedron equations), which are equivalent to the combined set of two tetrahedron equations and two octahedron equations.

In the next section, we introduce the
double zero property
, which states that the exterior derivative of a 2-form can be expanded such that each of its terms is a product of expressions that vanish on its corner equations. This will give us a variational interpretation for all of the equations discussed above, including the octahedron equations.

5
The double zero property of discrete exterior derivatives

To motivate the formulation of the double zero property, we first present some observations regarding a linear quad equation. Then we formalise the definition of a double zero expansion and provide constructions of double zero expansions for each of the 2-forms for any member of ABS list. Afterwards we investigate explicit expansions associated with each of the 2-forms for H1.

5.1
Double zero expansion associated with a linear quad equation

We consider the linear quad equation
Q_{ij}=0
, with

\displaystyle Q_{ij}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j})(u_{i}-u_{j})-(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})(u-u_{ij}).

This equation can be considered as a linearisation of H1 and is associated to the following triangle Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

\displaystyle=u(u_{i}-u_{j})-\frac{\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}}{2(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})}(u-u_{ij})^{2}\,.

Its action over an elementary cube can be written as

\displaystyle S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(u_{k},u_{ki},u_{jk},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})+\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(u_{i},u_{ij},u_{ki},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})+\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(u_{j},u_{jk},u_{ij},\alpha_{k},\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle\quad-\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(u,u_{i},u_{j},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})-\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(u,u_{j},u_{k},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})-\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(u,u_{k},u_{i},\alpha_{k},\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{O_{1}\,O_{2}}{(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})},

(48)

where

\displaystyle O_{1}

\displaystyle:=(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})u_{i}+(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})u_{jk}\,+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\,,

\displaystyle O_{2}

\displaystyle:=\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})u_{i}-\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})u_{jk}\,+{\circlearrowleft}_{ijk}\,.

Hence, the action over an elementary cube can be explicitly written as a product of two expressions
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
. They are symmetric under cyclic permutation of the indices and under point inversion, in the sense that
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,O\,}\!_{1}=O_{1}
and
\!\overleftrightarrow{\,O\,}\!_{2}=-O_{2}
.

In analogy to multiple zeros of polynomials, we say that
S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
has a
double zero
on the system of equations
O_{1}=0
,
O_{2}=0
.
The factorisation (
48
) implies that for any
v\in\{u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},u_{ijk}\}
there holds

\frac{\partial{S_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.91 0 L 0 0 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{\partial{v}}=\frac{1}{(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})}\left(\frac{\partial{O_{1}}}{\partial{v}}O_{2}+O_{1}\frac{\partial{O_{2}}}{\partial{v}}\right),

which is zero if both
O_{1}=0
and
O_{2}=0
. Hence, the double zero property tells us that the action is critical if the two equations
O_{1}=0
and
O_{2}=0
hold. This implies that all corner equations are consequences of these two equations!

This linear example shows the power of the double zero property: if
S
can be written as a product of two expressions, then the fact that both these expressions vanish is a sufficient condition for criticality. Hence, in this situation, two equations together imply the full system of corner equations.

In some examples, the double zero expansion of
S
involves more than two expressions and is a sum of products, rather than a single product. In the next subsection we give a suitably general definition of a double zero expansion.

5.2
Definition of a double zero Expansion

Definition 11
.

We say that
S(u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},u_{ijk})
has a
double zero
on a set of equations
\{K_{m}(u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},u_{ijk})=0\mid m=1,\ldots M\}
if it can be written as

S=\sum_{1\leq m\leq m^{\prime}\leq M}\,d_{m,m^{\prime}}\,K_{m}\,K_{m^{\prime}}\,.

(49)

Here,
d_{m,m^{\prime}}
are coefficients that can depend upon any variable or parameter, but must be nonsingular on generic points of
\{K_{m}(u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},u_{ijk})=0\mid m=1,\ldots M\}
.
We call the right hand side of Equation (
49
) a
double zero expansion
of
S
.

This definition applies to the example above, because Equation (
48
) is of the form (
49
) with
M=2
,
K_{1}=O_{1}
,
K_{2}=O_{2}
,
d_{1,1}=d_{2,2}=0
, and

d_{1,2}=\frac{1}{(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{k})(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{i})}\,.

Proposition 12
.

If the action over an elementary cube
S
of a Lagrangian 2-form
L
has a double zero on a system of equations
\{K_{m}=0\mid m=1,\ldots M\}
, then this system implies the corner equations of
L
.

Proof.

For any
v\in\{u,u_{i},u_{j},u_{k},u_{ij},u_{jk},u_{ki},u_{ijk}\}
we have

\frac{\partial{S}}{\partial{v}}=\sum_{1\leq m\leq m^{\prime}\leq M}\left(\frac{\partial{d_{m,m^{\prime}}}}{\partial{v}}K_{m}K_{m^{\prime}}+d_{m,m^{\prime}}\frac{\partial{K_{m}}}{\partial{v}}K_{m^{\prime}}+d_{m,m^{\prime}}K_{m}\frac{\partial{K_{m^{\prime}}}}{\partial{v}}\right),

which vanishes on
\{K_{m}=0\mid m=1,\ldots M\}
.
∎

Below, we will use Taylor expansions to derive double zero expansions involving higher powers of expressions. For example, consider an action
S
which is critical on some equation
K_{1}=0
, and an expansion of the form

S=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}c_{n}\,K_{1}^{n}\,.

This satisfies Definition
11
, because we can write the action as

S=\left(\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}c_{n}\,K_{1}^{n-2}\right)K_{1}^{2}\,,

where the sum can be identified with
d_{1,1}
.

5.3
Double zero expansions for ABS 2-forms

In this subsection we derive double zero expansions for the action over an elementary cube for each of the discrete 2-forms for arbitrary members of the ABS list. For ease of notation, we assume that we are in a case where the closure property holds:

Assumption 13
.

In the following we assume that
S^{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}}=0
(or
S^{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}=0
for H1, A1
δ=0
, Q1
δ=0
) on solutions. To drop this assumption, a multiple of
4\pi^{2}
should be added as constant term to the series expansions that follow.

The double zero expansions are constructed from Taylor expansions in one variable, where the variable represents either a quad polynomial
Q
, a tetrahedron polynomial
T
or an
E
polynomial. In this Taylor expansion, the zeroth order term vanishes due to the closure relation and the first order term vanishes as a consequence of the corner equations. Hence, each discrete 2-form has a double zero expansion in terms of the polynomials associated with its corner equations. These double zero expansions are manifestly symmetric under cyclic permutation of the indices and under point inversion.

Cross-square 2-form.

Recall that
Q_{ij}=0
implies that
\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 5.91 0 L 0 5.91 L 0 0 L 5.91 0 L 5.91 5.91 L 0 5.91 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=0
, , where

denotes the gradient with respect to

(see Lemma

)
and that

). To get a more explicit picture of the behaviour of

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and
S^{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Xi}}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=0
near solutions, we would like to perform a Taylor expansion about solutions
Q_{ij}=0
. To this end, it is useful to consider
Q_{ij}
as one of the variables on which
\mathcal{L}_{\hbox to4.67pt{\vbox to4.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
